WHY -— To prevent the enemy from using or salvaging this equipment.

WHEN — When ordered by your commander.

HOW  — 1. SmashU—se sledges, axes, handaxes, pickaxes, hammers, crow-
                            bars, heavy tools.

     2. Cu—tUse axes, handaxes, machetes.

     3. Bur—nUse gasoline,      kerosene,  oil, flame  throwers,   incendiary
                     grenades.

     4. Explosiv—eUsse firearms, grenades, TNT.

     5. Disposal — Bury in slit trenches, fox holes, other hoies.

                             Throw in streams. Scatter.

 USE ANYTHING IMMEDIATELY AVAILABLE FOR DESTRUCTION
                               OF THIS EQUIPMENT.

WHAT — |. Sma — s Receh iver, frequency meter, recorder, amplifier.
                    Z. Cut — Coaxial lines, cables, cords.

                                                       equipment, this control is used to produce equal

60. Dustproofing
                                                                                                                               37
Signal Corps equipment operated in desert localities
is affected by the extremely high temperatures and
the amount of dirt, dust, sand, and other foreign
matter in the air. Be careful to prevent such elements
from filtering into lubricated parts. Cover the equip-
ment when it is not in use. Thorough cleanliness is
imperative. Instead of merely adding new lubricants
at regular intervals, whenever practicable clean and

   g. VOLTAGE RecuLator. The voltage regulator
maintains the a-c power input to the power supplies
of the frequency meter and amplifier at a constant
voltage (0.5%) even though wide variations of
line voltage occur. The regulator has two trans-
formers and two capacitors in a suitable circuit to
perform this function.

69. Antennas and Transmission Lines                                       UPPER LEG
                                                          My
Part of the electromagnetic energy radiated by the

radiosonde antenna, after travelling through space,

sweeps across the receiving antenna at the velocity
of light, inducing in the receiving antenna a voltage
which is proportional to the strength of the travel-
ling electromagnetic wave. At frequencies of 72.2

me and 403 mc, this energy is propagated in a                     |    INSULATOR
direct line, commonly called line of sight transmis-
sion and suffers little effect by absorption in the at-             }

mosphere of the earth or in the ground. This direct                |

wave, however, is subject to reflection, and objects                |
in the path of the wave can deflect it and change its
                                                                  |
direction of travel. Thus, if a tree, building, or other
object obstructs the line of sight path between the       | |

antenna of the radiosonde and the antenna of the          w ||
radiosonde receptor, the induced voltage may be
                                                           | |
attenuated or lost completely. For this reason, two

antennas are installed for each receptor, thus insur-
ing, at any azimuth angle, an unobstructed line of

sight path between the radiosonde antenna and one

d. Capacitor C602 and resistor R600 form an
                                                                                                                      61
R-C coupling network. The values of the capacitor

and resistor in this network have been chosen so

that the waveform at the grid of the tube (with the

tube removed from its socket) is essentially the

same as the input voltage. A linear R-C network

at the very low audio frequencies used would

require extremely large values for the capacitor and

resistor and, (because the size of the grid resistor

is limited by the tube characteristics) the use of a

linear network is not practical.

e. Bias voltage is obtained from the voltage drop

across cathode resistor R602. C600 is the cathode

a-f bypass capacitor.             .

f. Figure 37 shows waveforms at the input of

the frequency meter and at the grid of the tube

for operation at 40- and 180-cycle input frequencies.

Note that the positive peaks of the input wave are

flattened as a result of grid limiting. At the higher

grid voltages, the grid becomes more positive than

the cathode, and grid current flows, at which time

the grid voltage cannot rise to any appreciable

extent. Note that on the negative alternation of the

40-cycle grid voltage, the lagging edge is rounded.

This distortion is caused by the small R-C values

   b. TROUBLE-SHOOTING STEPs. The first step in         the electrical accuracy of the voltage measurement
servicing a defective radio set is to sectionalize the
fault. Sectionalization means tracing the fault to
the component or circuit responsible for the ab-
normal operation of the set. The second step is to
localize the fault. Localization means tracing the
fault to the defective part responsible for the ab- |
normal condition. Some faults such as burned-out
resistors, r-f arcing, and shorted transformers can
often be located by sight, smell, and hearing. The
majority of faults, however, must be located by
checking voltage and resistance.

   (1) Sectionalization. Careful observation of the
performance of the radio set during the starting
procedure often sectionalizes the fault to the trans-
